--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are F (black) and D (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect F and D.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node F is connected to G, K, X, and P.
    *   Node D is connected to J and P.

2.  **Analyze paths to D:**
    Any path from F to D must eventually reach D through one of its direct connections. In this graph, D is only connected to node J and node P.

3.  **Consider the edges directly connected to D:**
    *   Edge (P, D)
    *   Edge (J, D)

4.  **Evaluate the impact of removing these edges:**
    *   If we remove only (P, D), then paths from F to D that go through P are broken (e.g., F-P-D). However, paths from F to D that go through J would still exist (e.g., F-G-H-I-J-D).
    *   If we remove only (J, D), then paths from F to D that go through J are broken. However, paths from F to D that go through P would still exist (e.g., F-P-D).

5.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    To completely disconnect D from F, both of its incoming/outgoing edges must be removed. If both (P, D) and (J, D) are removed, node D becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between F and D.
    This set of edges, [(P, D), (J, D)], has a size of 2.
    Since D has two distinct connections, it is impossible to disconnect it from the rest of the graph (and thus from F) by removing only one edge. Therefore, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(P, D), (J, D)]

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(K, [K])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{K}`

2.  **Level 1 (Paths of length 1 from K):**
    *   Dequeue `(K, [K])`.
    *   Neighbors of K: F, G, X.
    *   Add `(F, [K, F])`, `(G, [K, G])`, `(X, [K, X])` to the queue.
    *   Visited: `{K, F, G, X}`
    *   Queue: `[(F, [K, F]), (G, [K, G]), (X, [K, X])]`

3.  **Level 2 (Paths of length 2 from K):**
    *   Dequeue `(F, [K, F])`.
        *   Neighbors of F: K (visited), G (visited), E.
        *   Add `(E, [K, F, E])` to the queue.
        *   Visited: `{K, F, G, X, E}`
    *   Dequeue `(G, [K, G])`.
        *   Neighbors of G: K (visited), F (visited), X (visited), H.
        *   Add `(H, [K, G, H])` to the queue.
        *   Visited: `{K, F, G, X, E, H}`
    *   Dequeue `(X, [K, X])`.
        *   Neighbors of X: K (visited), G (visited). No new unvisited nodes.
    *   Queue: `[(E, [K, F, E]), (H, [K, G, H])]`

4.  **Level 3 (Paths of length 3 from K):**
    *   Dequeue `(E, [K, F, E])`.
        *   Neighbors of E: F (visited), H (visited), L, M.
        *   Add `(L, [K, F, E, L])`, `(M, [K, F, E, M])` to the queue.
        *   Visited: `{K, F, G, X, E, H, L, M}`
    *   Dequeue `(H, [K, G, H])`.
        *   Neighbors of H: G (visited), E (visited), B, I.
        *   Add `(B, [K, G, H, B])`, `(I, [K, G, H, I])` to the queue.
        *   Visited: `{K, F, G, X, E, H, L, M, B, I}`
    *   Queue: `[(L, [K, F, E, L]), (M, [K, F, E, M]), (B, [K, G, H, B]), (I, [K, G, H, I])]`

5.  **Level 4 (Paths of length 4 from K):**
    *   Dequeue `(L, [K, F, E, L])`.
        *   Neighbors of L: E (visited), M (visited). No new unvisited nodes.
    *   Dequeue `(M, [K, F, E, M])`.
        *   Neighbors of M: E (visited), L (visited), J.
        *   Add `(J, [K, F, E, M, J])` to the queue.
        *   Visited: `{K, F, G, X, E, H, L, M, B, I, J}`
    *   Dequeue `(B, [K, G, H, B])`.
        *   Neighbors of B: H (visited), C, N.
        *   Add `(C, [K, G, H, B, C])` to the queue.
        *   **Node N is the target node!** The path found is `[K, G, H, B, N]`.
        *   Since this is a breadth-first search, the first time the target node is reached guarantees that this is the shortest path.

6.  **Verify the path:**
    *   K is connected to G. (Yes)
    *   G is connected to H. (Yes)
    *   H is connected to B. (Yes)
    *   B is connected to N. (Yes)

The path `[K, G, H, B, N]` is valid and has a length of 4 edges.

Image name: PG_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and B (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and B.

First, I list all direct neighbors of H and B to understand their immediate connections:
- Neighbors of H: I, G, X
- Neighbors of B: E, N, C, O

Next, I identify all possible paths from H to B. I can categorize them based on the first edge from H:

**Paths starting with (H, I):**
1.  H - I - C - B
2.  H - I - C - N - B
3.  H - I - J - M - O - B
4.  H - I - J - P - O - B

**Paths starting with (H, G):**
5.  H - G - X - F - E - B
6.  H - G - K - F - E - B
7.  H - G - F - E - B

**Paths starting with (H, X):**
8.  H - X - F - E - B
9.  H - X - K - F - E - B

Now, I look for a minimum cut set.
A single edge removal cannot disconnect H from B because both H and B have multiple connections. For example, if I remove (H, I), paths like H-G-F-E-B still exist. If I remove (E, B), paths like H-I-C-B still exist. Therefore, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

Let's try to find a cut set of size 2.
I observe that all paths starting with (H, G) or (H, X) eventually converge and pass through the sequence of nodes F, E, and then connect to B via the edge (E, B). This means that removing the edge (E, B) would effectively cut all paths originating from H through G or X.

If I remove the edge (E, B), the following paths are broken:
- H - G - X - F - E - B
- H - G - K - F - E - B
- H - G - F - E - B
- H - X - F - E - B
- H - X - K - F - E - B

After removing (E, B), the only remaining paths from H to B are those that start with (H, I):
- H - I - C - B
- H - I - C - N - B
- H - I - J - M - O - B
- H - I - J - P - O - B

To break these remaining paths, I need to remove an edge that is common to all of them. The most direct way to cut all these paths is to remove the initial edge (H, I).

Let's verify the proposed cut set: [(H, I), (E, B)].
1.  **Remove (H, I):** This immediately breaks all paths that start with H-I (paths 1, 2, 3, 4).
2.  **Remove (E, B):** This breaks all paths that end with E-B (paths 5, 6, 7, 8, 9).

Since all paths from H to B are covered by these two edge removals, H and B are completely disconnected.
This cut set has a size of 2. As established earlier, a cut set of size 1 is not possible. Therefore, 2 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, I), (E, B)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['B', 'H'], ['H', 'I'], ['G', 'H']]}

Image name: Tu_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and E.

2.  **List all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to C, I, and G.
    *   Node E is connected to P, L, M, and B.

3.  **Trace all possible paths from H to E:**
    *   **Paths starting H-C:**
        *   H-C-B-E
        *   H-C-N-O-P-E
        *   H-C-N-Y-O-P-E (This path is longer but uses the same critical edges as H-C-N-O-P-E to reach E)
    *   **Paths starting H-I:**
        *   H-I-P-E
        *   H-I-J-M-E
    *   **Paths starting H-G:**
        *   H-G-F-P-E
        *   H-G-K-F-P-E (Uses F-P-E)
        *   H-G-X-F-P-E (Uses F-P-E)

4.  **Identify potential cut edges:** To disconnect H from E, we need to break all these paths. A common strategy is to look at the edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that are part of paths from the other target node.
    The edges connected to E are (P, E), (L, E), (M, E), and (B, E).
    *   The edge (L, E) is not part of any path originating from H, as L is only connected to E and M, and M is only connected to J and E. There's no path from H to L. So, (L, E) does not need to be considered for disconnecting H from E.
    *   The relevant edges connected to E for paths from H are (P, E), (M, E), and (B, E).

5.  **Propose a cut set and verify:**
    Let's consider removing the edges (B, E), (P, E), and (M, E).
    *   Removing (B, E) breaks the path H-C-B-E.
    *   Removing (P, E) breaks all paths that go through P to E: H-C-N-O-P-E, H-C-N-Y-O-P-E, H-I-P-E, H-G-F-P-E, H-G-K-F-P-E, H-G-X-F-P-E.
    *   Removing (M, E) breaks the path H-I-J-M-E.

    After removing these three edges, all identified paths from H to E are broken. The size of this cut set is 3.

6.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (minimum verification):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we can look for edge-disjoint paths between H and E. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then at least *k* edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes (by Menger's Theorem).
    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H-C-B-E (Edges: (H,C), (C,B), (B,E))
    *   **Path 2:** H-I-J-M-E (Edges: (H,I), (I,J), (J,M), (M,E))
    *   **Path 3:** H-G-F-P-E (Edges: (H,G), (G,F), (F,P), (P,E))

    These three paths are completely edge-disjoint (they do not share any common edges). Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H and E.
    Since we found a cut set of size 3, and we've proven that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    An alternative minimal cut set of size 3 would be to remove all edges directly connected to H: [(H, C), (H, I), (H, G)]. This would also isolate H from the rest of the graph, and thus from E.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (P, E), (M, E)]

{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']]}

Image name: SEIS_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Identify direct neighbors of target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of L: E, M
    *   Neighbors of J: M, K, I, P, D
3.  **Understand the goal:** Disconnect L from J by removing the minimum number of non-target nodes.
4.  **Strategy: Find vertex-disjoint paths.** According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.
    *   A path is a sequence of nodes where each consecutive pair is directly linked.
    *   Vertex-disjoint paths are paths that share no common *intermediate* vertices (i.e., nodes other than the start and end nodes).

5.  **Find vertex-disjoint paths from L to J:**
    *   **Path 1:** L -> M -> J
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M}
    *   **Path 2:** Now, we look for a path from L to J that does not use M as an intermediate node.
        *   L -> E -> G -> K -> J
        *   Intermediate nodes: {E, G, K}

6.  **Check for disjointness:** The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {M}. The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {E, G, K}. These two sets are disjoint. Therefore, Path 1 and Path 2 are vertex-disjoint.

7.  **Attempt to find a third vertex-disjoint path:**
    *   Any path from L must start with L-E or L-M.
    *   M is already used as an intermediate node in Path 1.
    *   E is already used as an intermediate node in Path 2.
    *   Since all initial branches from L (L-E and L-M) lead to nodes already used as intermediate nodes in the first two paths, it is impossible to construct a third vertex-disjoint path.

8.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** Since we found a maximum of 2 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect L and J is 2.

9.  **Identify a cut set of size 2:** To break both Path 1 and Path 2, we need to remove at least one intermediate node from each path.
    *   To break Path 1 (L-M-J), we must remove M.
    *   To break Path 2 (L-E-G-K-J), we must remove E, G, or K.
    *   If we choose to remove M and E:
        *   Removing M breaks L-M-J.
        *   Removing E breaks L-E-G-K-J.
        *   Furthermore, L's only neighbors are E and M. If both E and M are removed, L becomes completely isolated from all other nodes in the graph (except J, which is the target). Thus, L is disconnected from J.

10. **Verify the cut set [M, E]:**
    *   If M is removed, the path L-M-J is broken.
    *   If E is removed, all paths starting with L-E-... are broken.
    *   Since L only connects to M and E, removing both M and E ensures that L has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph, thus disconnecting it from J.
    *   No smaller set of nodes (i.e., a single node) can disconnect L from J, as demonstrated by the existence of two vertex-disjoint paths.

The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, E]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'M']}

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Identify Immediate Connections:**
    *   Node L is directly connected to E and M.
    *   Node J is directly connected to P, I, M, and D.
3.  **Analyze Paths:**
    Any path from L to J must pass through either node E or node M (since these are L's only neighbors).
    Let's list some initial paths:
    *   **Path 1:** L - M - J (This is a direct path through M)
    *   **Path 2:** L - E - P - J (This path goes through E and P)
    *   **Path 3:** L - E - P - I - J (This path goes through E, P, and I)
    *   **Path 4:** L - E - B - C - I - J (This path goes through E, B, C, and I)
    *   ... and many other paths involving nodes like H, O, N, etc.
4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:**
    *   **Can we disconnect L and J by removing only one node?**
        *   If we remove M, Path 1 (L-M-J) is broken. However, paths starting with L-E-...-J (like L-E-P-J) still exist.
        *   If we remove E, paths starting with L-E-...-J are broken. However, Path 1 (L-M-J) still exists.
        *   If we remove P, paths L-M-J, L-E-B-...-J, etc., still exist.
        *   If we remove I, paths L-M-J, L-E-P-J, L-E-B-O-P-J, etc., still exist.
        *   If we remove D, D is only connected to J. Removing D does not disconnect L from J.
        Since removing any single node leaves at least one path between L and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
5.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    Based on the observation that all paths from L must go through either E or M, consider removing both E and M.
    *   If node M is removed, L is no longer connected to M. L is now only connected to E.
    *   If node E is also removed, L is no longer connected to E.
    *   With both M and E removed, node L has no remaining connections to any other white nodes in the graph. Therefore, L is completely disconnected from J.
    This set of nodes [M, E] successfully disconnects L from J.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum cut size is at least 2, and we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, E]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and E (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that, when removed, will completely disconnect H from E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** H and E.

2.  **Explore paths between H and E:** I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. I will systematically search for edge-disjoint paths from H to E.

    *   **Path 1:** H - I - J - M - L - E
        Edges used: (H,I), (I,J), (J,M), (M,L), (L,E)

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - K - F - E
        Edges used: (H,G), (G,K), (K,F), (F,E)
        Let's check if Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1.
        (H,G) is not (H,I). (G,K) is not (I,J), (J,M), (M,L), (L,E). (K,F) is not any edge in Path 1. (F,E) is not any edge in Path 1.
        So, Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Now I need to find a third path that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
        The remaining direct connection from H is (H,X). Let's try to build a path from H through X.
        H - X - F - B - E
        Edges used: (H,X), (X,F), (F,B), (B,E)
        Let's check if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
        -   **Vs Path 1:** (H,X) is not (H,I). (X,F) is not (I,J), (J,M), (M,L), (L,E). (F,B) is not any edge in Path 1. (B,E) is not any edge in Path 1. Disjoint from Path 1.
        -   **Vs Path 2:** (H,X) is not (H,G). (X,F) is not (G,K), (K,F), (F,E). (F,B) is not (G,K), (K,F), (F,E). (B,E) is not (G,K), (K,F), (F,E). Disjoint from Path 2.
        So, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

3.  **Conclusion on edge-disjoint paths:** I have successfully identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E.
    *   Path 1: H - I - J - M - L - E
    *   Path 2: H - G - K - F - E
    *   Path 3: H - X - F - B - E

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** According to Menger's theorem, since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and E is 3.

5.  **Identify a minimal cut set:** To disconnect H from E, I need to remove at least one edge from each of these 3 edge-disjoint paths. A straightforward way to achieve this is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, provided that node has a degree equal to the minimum cut size.
    *   Node H has three outgoing edges: (H, I), (H, G), (H, X). If these three edges are removed, H becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist from H to E. This set has 3 edges.
    *   Alternatively, node E has three incoming edges: (B, E), (F, E), (L, E). If these three edges are removed, E becomes an isolated node, and no path can reach E from H. This set also has 3 edges.

    Both sets are valid minimal cut sets of size 3. I will choose the one connected to H.

6.  **Verification:** Removing edges [(H, I), (H, G), (H, X)] isolates node H from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from H to E. This confirms the disconnection. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, a cut set of size 3 is indeed minimal.

The final answer is structured as requested.

*   Removing B will break all paths that use B to reach E (e.g., H-C-N-B-E).

    Therefore, a two-node removal is not sufficient.

Since removing any single node or any pair of nodes from {M, B, F} leaves at least one path open, I must remove all three nodes: M, B, and F. This set of nodes completely severs all connections from H to E.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and H. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and H. I cannot remove E or H.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of H, as any path to H must pass through one of these nodes.
The neighbors of H are: G, B, I.

Now, I trace all possible paths from E to H:
1.  **Path through B:** E - B - H
2.  **Path through I:** E - I - H
3.  **Path through G:** E - F - G - H (Note: E is connected to F, F is connected to G, and G is connected to H)

These three paths represent all distinct ways to reach H from E. To disconnect E and H, I must ensure that all these paths are broken.

Let's consider the nodes that would break these paths:
*   To break E - B - H, I must remove B.
*   To break E - I - H, I must remove I.
*   To break E - F - G - H, I must remove F or G.

Consider a cut set of size 1:
*   If I remove G: Paths E-B-H and E-I-H still exist.
*   If I remove B: Paths E-I-H and E-F-G-H still exist.
*   If I remove I: Paths E-B-H and E-F-G-H still exist.
*   If I remove F: Paths E-B-H and E-I-H still exist.
*   Removing any other single node (e.g., L, M, J, C, N, O, P, D, K, X, Y) would leave multiple paths intact.
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

Consider a cut set of size 2:
*   If I remove {G, B}: Path E-I-H still exists.
*   If I remove {G, I}: Path E-B-H still exists.
*   If I remove {B, I}: Path E-F-G-H still exists.
*   If I remove {F, B}: Path E-I-H still exists.
*   If I remove {F, I}: Path E-B-H still exists.
*   If I remove {G, F}: Paths E-B-H and E-I-H still exist. (This is the same as removing G and F, which breaks E-F-G-H, but leaves the other two).
In all these cases, at least one path remains. Therefore, a two-node removal is not sufficient.

Consider a cut set of size 3:
The nodes G, B, and I are the only nodes directly connected to H. If all these nodes are removed, H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph (excluding E, which cannot be removed).
*   Removing G breaks the path E-F-G-H.
*   Removing B breaks the path E-B-H.
*   Removing I breaks the path E-I-H.

By removing G, B, and I, all paths from E to H are severed. Since G, B, and I are the only neighbors of H, removing them disconnects H from the entire graph, thus also from E. This set of nodes is minimal because, as shown above, removing any two of them leaves at least one path intact.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [G, B, I]

*   Neighbors of G: X, K, F, H
*   Neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y

I need to find a set of edges that, when removed, disconnects G from N. This is a minimum s-t cut problem. I will systematically explore paths and potential cut sets.

**1. Initial Observation of N's Connections:**
Node N is directly connected to B, C, O, and Y. If I remove all four edges connected to N: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O), (N, Y), then N would be completely isolated. This would be a cut of size 4. I need to check if a smaller cut is possible.

**2. Identifying "Entry Points" to N's Cluster:**
To reach N, any path from G must eventually pass through one of N's direct neighbors: B, C, O, or Y.
*   Y is a "leaf" node in the context of reaching N from G, as it's only connected to N and O. If paths to O are cut, paths to Y are also effectively cut (from G's perspective).
*   Therefore, the critical nodes to disconnect from G are B, C, and O.

Let's identify the edges that serve as "bridges" from the "G-side" of the graph to the "N-side" (specifically, to B, C, and O).

*   **Paths to B:**
    *   From H: (H, B)
    *   From E: (E, B)
    These are the only two edges connecting to B from the "left" side of the graph (G's general direction).

*   **Paths to C:**
    *   From I: (I, C)
    (Note: (B, C) and (O, C) are internal to the {B, C, O, Y} cluster if B and O are already connected to G. We are looking for edges that connect *into* this cluster from G's side.)

*   **Paths to O:**
    *   From P: (P, O)
    (Note: (C, O) and (Y, O) are internal to the {B, C, O, Y} cluster if C and Y are already connected to G. P is the only node connecting to O from the "left" side of the graph.)

**3. Proposed Cut Set of 4 Edges:**
Based on the analysis above, the following four edges appear to be critical "entry points" into the N-cluster:
1.  (H, B)
2.  (E, B)
3.  (I, C)
4.  (P, O)

Let's verify if removing these four edges disconnects G from N:
*   **Disconnecting B from G:** Removing (H, B) and (E, B) ensures that no path from G can reach B.
*   **Disconnecting C from G:** Removing (I, C) cuts all paths from G that go through I to C. Since B is already disconnected from G, paths like G-...-B-C-N are also broken. The only other connection to C from the N-cluster is O.
*   **Disconnecting O from G:** Removing (P, O) cuts all paths from G that go through P to O. P is the only node connecting to O from the "left" side of the graph. Therefore, after removing (P, O), O is disconnected from G.
*   **Disconnecting Y from G:** Y is only connected to N and O. Since O is disconnected from G, Y is also disconnected from G.

With B, C, and O all disconnected from G, and Y only connected to N and O (which is disconnected from G), there are no remaining paths from G to N. This cut set of 4 edges is effective.

**4. Attempting a Smaller Cut (3 Edges):**
Now, I need to determine if a cut of size 3 is possible. I will try to remove any three of the four critical edges identified above and check if a path still exists.

*   **Case 1: Remove [(H, B), (E, B), (I, C)]**
    *   (P, O) remains.
    *   A path like G-H-I-P-O-N still exists. (H-I is not cut, I-P is not cut, P-O is not cut, O-N is not cut).
    *   Therefore, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

*   **Case 2: Remove [(H, B), (E, B), (P, O)]**
    *   (I, C) remains.
    *   A path like G-H-I-C-N still exists. (H-I is not cut, I-C is not cut, C-N is not cut).
    *   Therefore, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

*   **Case 3: Remove [(H, B), (I, C), (P, O)]**
    *   (E, B) remains.
    *   A path like G-F-E-B-N still exists. (F-E is not cut, E-B is not cut, B-N is not cut).
    *   Therefore, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   (H, B) remains.
    *   A path like G-H-B-N still exists. (H-B is not cut, B-N is not cut).
    *   Therefore, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

Since removing any combination of 3 edges from this critical set of 4 leaves at least one path open, and these 4 edges represent the primary "entry points" from the G-side to the N-side, it confirms that 3 edges are not enough.

**Conclusion:**
The minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G from N is 4.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, B), (E, B), (I, C), (P, O)]

3.  **Path 3: H - E - B**
    Edges: `{(H, E), (E, B)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, as it uses no common edges.

4.  **Path 4: H - G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - N - B**
    Edges: `{(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B)}`
    Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from Paths 1, 2, and 3:
    *   Path 1: `(H, B)` - No common edges.
    *   Path 2: `(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)` - No common edges.
    *   Path 3: `(H, E), (E, B)` - No common edges. (Note: Path 4 uses (F,E) and (E,L), while Path 3 uses (H,E) and (E,B). These are distinct edges.)
    Therefore, this is a fourth edge-disjoint path.

Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 4.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that actually disconnects H and B. A common strategy is to define a cut (S, T) where H is in set S and B is in set T, and then identify all edges crossing this cut.

Let's define the set S = {H, G, F, X, K} and T = {B, I, C, E, N, J, P, O, M, L, D, Y}.
The edges that connect a node in S to a node in T are:
*   **(H, B)** (H in S, B in T)
*   **(H, I)** (H in S, I in T)
*   **(H, E)** (H in S, E in T)
*   **(F, E)** (F in S, E in T)

These are the only edges connecting a node in S to a node in T. If these 4 edges are removed, there will be no path from H (which is in S) to B (which is in T).

Let's verify that removing these 4 edges `[(H, B), (H, I), (H, E), (F, E)]` disconnects H from B:
1.  Any path starting `H - B` is broken by removing `(H, B)`.
2.  Any path starting `H - I - ...` is broken by removing `(H, I)`.
3.  Any path starting `H - E - ...` is broken by removing `(H, E)`.
4.  Any path starting `H - G - ...` or `H - F - ...` must eventually leave the set {H, G, F, X, K} to reach B. The only edge connecting this set to the rest of the graph (which contains B) is `(F, E)`. Since `(F, E)` is removed, no path can leave this component to reach B.

All paths from H to B are broken by removing these 4 edges. Since we found 4 edge-disjoint paths, and a cut set of size 4, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, I), (H, E), (F, E)]

    *   Node B is connected to H, N, C, and E.

2.  **Consider the edges directly connected to H:**
    Any path from B to H must eventually use one of the edges connected to H. These edges are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H). If we remove all three of these edges, node H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This would be a cut of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible (e.g., size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Cut of size 1:** If we remove only one edge (e.g., (B, H)), there are clearly still paths from B to H (e.g., B-C-I-H or B-E-F-G-H). So, 1 edge is not enough.
    *   **Cut of size 2:** Let's assume we can disconnect B and H by removing only two edges. This means all paths from B to H must pass through these two specific edges.
        *   If we remove (B, H) and (G, H), a path like B-C-I-H still exists.
        *   If we remove (B, H) and (I, H), a path like B-E-F-G-H still exists.
        *   If we remove (G, H) and (I, H), the direct path B-H still exists.
        Since removing any two of the edges directly connected to H still leaves at least one path, a cut of size 2 is not sufficient if we only consider these three edges.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Min-Cut Max-Flow):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from B to H.

    *   **Path 1:** B - H (uses edge (B, H))
    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H (uses edges (B, C), (C, I), (I, H))
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as none of its edges are (B, H).
    *   **Path 3:** B - E - F - G - H (uses edges (B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H))
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (no (B, H)).
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 2 (no (B, C), (C, I), (I, H)).

    We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect B and H must be at least 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found a cut set of size 3 (by removing all edges connected to H: (B, H), (G, H), (I, H)), and we have proven that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are N and P. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N from P. I cannot remove N or P.

First, I will identify all possible paths between N and P. To simplify, I will consider the direct neighbors of N and P.
Neighbors of N: {Y, O, C, B}
Neighbors of P: {O, I, E, J, F}

I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between N and P. If I can find `k` vertex-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum vertex cut size is at least `k`.

Let's trace some paths:
1.  **Path 1: N - O - P**
    *   Intermediate node: {O}

2.  **Path 2: N - B - I - P**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, I}

3.  **Path 3: N - C - H - G - F - P**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {C, H, G, F}

Let's check if the sets of intermediate nodes for these three paths are disjoint:
*   Set 1: {O}
*   Set 2: {B, I}
*   Set 3: {C, H, G, F}

These three sets are mutually disjoint. This means that these three paths are vertex-disjoint (except for the start and end nodes N and P).
Therefore, according to Menger's theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect N from P.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3. I will try to identify three nodes that effectively block all paths. Based on the disjoint paths identified, I can try to pick one node from each path's intermediate set. For example, {O}, {B}, {H}.

Let's test the cut set {O, B, H}:
1.  **Remove O:** This severs all paths that go through O.
    *   Direct path N-O-P is cut.
    *   Paths like N-Y-O-..., N-C-O-..., N-B-C-O-... are cut.
    *   Paths to P via E that use O (e.g., N-O-E-P, N-Y-O-E-P, N-C-O-E-P, N-B-C-O-E-P) are cut.
    *   Paths to P via I that use O (e.g., N-O-C-B-I-P, N-O-C-H-I-P) are cut.
    *   Paths to P via F that use O (e.g., N-O-C-H-G-F-P) are cut.

2.  **Remove B:** This severs all paths that go through B.
    *   Paths like N-B-I-P and N-B-E-P are cut.
    *   Paths like N-C-B-I-P and N-C-B-E-P are cut.
    *   Paths like N-B-C-... (e.g., N-B-C-O-P, N-B-C-H-I-P, N-B-C-H-G-F-P) are cut.

3.  **Remove H:** This severs all paths that go through H.
    *   Paths like N-C-H-I-P and N-C-H-G-F-P are cut.
    *   Paths like N-Y-O-C-H-..., N-O-C-H-..., N-B-C-H-... are cut.

Now, let's verify that no path remains from N to P after removing {O, B, H}:

*   **Paths to P via O:** All paths to O are cut by removing O.
*   **Paths to P via I:**
    *   Any path N-...-I-P must pass through B, C, or H to reach I.
    *   N-B-I-P is cut by removing B.
    *   N-C-B-I-P is cut by removing B.
    *   N-C-H-I-P is cut by removing H.
    *   Any path through Y or O to C or B, then to I, would be cut by O or B or H.
    *   All paths to I are cut.
*   **Paths to P via E:**
    *   Any path N-...-E-P must pass through O or B to reach E (or M then E, but M is connected to E and L, and L is not connected to N's side).
    *   N-O-E-P is cut by removing O.
    *   N-B-E-P is cut by removing B.
    *   N-C-B-E-P is cut by removing B.
    *   Any path through Y or O to C or B, then to E, would be cut by O or B.
    *   All paths to E are cut.
*   **Paths to P via J:**
    *   Any path N-...-J-P must pass through M. M is connected to E and L.
    *   Since all paths to E are cut (as verified above), M is disconnected from N's side (L is not connected to N's side).
    *   Therefore, all paths to J are cut.
*   **Paths to P via F:**
    *   Any path N-...-F-P must pass through G. G is connected to H, K, X.
    *   N-C-H-G-F-P is cut by removing H.
    *   N-B-C-H-G-F-P is cut by removing B or H.
    *   N-O-C-H-G-F-P is cut by removing O or H.
    *   N-Y-O-C-H-G-F-P is cut by removing O or H.
    *   All paths to G are cut, and thus all paths to F are cut.

Since all paths from N to P are severed by removing {O, B, H}, and we have established that at least 3 nodes are required, this is the minimum cut set.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [O, B, H]

    Node M is directly connected to three other nodes: E, J, and L. This means any path from B to M must pass through one of these three nodes and then use the corresponding edge to reach M. The edges are (E, M), (J, M), and (L, M).

2.  **Consider removing edges directly connected to M:**
    *   If I remove only one edge (e.g., (E, M)), paths through J (e.g., B-I-P-J-M) and L (e.g., B-E-L-M) would still exist. So, 1 edge is not enough.
    *   If I remove any two edges (e.g., (E, M) and (J, M)), the path through L (B-E-L-M) would still exist.
        *   Path B-E-L-M: B is connected to E, E is connected to L, L is connected to M. If (E, M) and (J, M) are removed, this path remains.
        *   Similarly, if (E, M) and (L, M) are removed, paths through J (e.g., B-I-P-J-M) remain.
        *   If (J, M) and (L, M) are removed, paths through E (e.g., B-E-M) remain.
    *   Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to M is not sufficient to disconnect B from M.

3.  **Consider removing three edges directly connected to M:**
    If I remove all three edges directly connected to M: (E, M), (J, M), and (L, M).
    *   (E, M) is removed: M cannot be reached directly from E.
    *   (J, M) is removed: M cannot be reached directly from J.
    *   (L, M) is removed: M cannot be reached directly from L.
    Since M has no other connections, removing these three edges completely isolates M from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

4.  **Explore alternative cut sets of size 3 (to confirm minimality):**
    Let's consider if there's another way to cut all paths with 3 edges, potentially by cutting edges further away from M.
    *   All paths from B to J must pass through P (e.g., B-I-P-J-M). So, removing (P, J) would cut all paths to J.
    *   All paths from B to L must pass through E (e.g., B-E-L-M). So, removing (E, L) would cut all paths to L.
    *   After removing (P, J) and (E, L), M can only be reached via E directly. Paths like B-E-M, B-C-I-P-E-M, etc., still exist. To cut these, we need to remove (E, M).
    *   This gives an alternative cut set of size 3: [(P, J), (E, L), (E, M)]. This also successfully disconnects B from M.

5.  **Conclusion on minimum number of edges:**
    Since I have systematically shown that 1 or 2 edges are insufficient, and I have found multiple valid cut sets of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (J, M), (L, M)]

    *   **Path B:** H - G - B. Edges: {(H, G), (G, B)}
    *   **Path C:** H - I - C - B. Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)}

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and D. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and D. I cannot remove E or D.

First, I identify the direct neighbors of the target node D. These are J and P.
Any path from E to D must pass through either J or P.

Next, I identify the nodes that are directly connected to J or P, but are not D itself. These nodes are the "gateways" to the D-side of the graph.
*   Neighbors of J (excluding D): I, M, P.
*   Neighbors of P (excluding D): I, J, O.

Combining these, any path from E to D must pass through at least one of the nodes {I, M, O} to reach J or P, and then D. Let's verify this:
*   If a path reaches D via J (e.g., E -> ... -> X -> J -> D), then X must be I, M, or P.
    *   If X is I, then I is part of the cut.
    *   If X is M, then M is part of the cut.
    *   If X is P, then the path must have reached P first (E -> ... -> Y -> P -> J -> D). Y must be I, J, or O. If Y is I or O, then I or O is part of the cut. If Y is J, then it's a cycle P-J-P, which means the path must have reached J first (E -> ... -> Z -> J -> P -> J -> D). Z must be I or M. So I or M is part of the cut.
*   If a path reaches D via P (e.g., E -> ... -> X -> P -> D), then X must be I, J, or O.
    *   If X is I, then I is part of the cut.
    *   If X is O, then O is part of the cut.
    *   If X is J, then the path must have reached J first (E -> ... -> Y -> J -> P -> D). Y must be I, M, or P. If Y is I or M, then I or M is part of the cut. If Y is P, then it's a cycle J-P-J, which means the path must have reached P first (E -> ... -> Z -> P -> J -> P -> D). Z must be I or O. So I or O is part of the cut.

This confirms that any path from E to D must pass through at least one of the nodes {M, I, O}. Therefore, removing all three of these nodes will disconnect E from D. This gives a cut set of size 3.

Now, I need to check if a smaller cut set (e.g., 1 or 2 nodes) is possible.

**Can we disconnect E and D by removing 1 node?**
No, because there are multiple independent paths that do not share a single common intermediate node. For example:
*   Path 1: E -> M -> J -> D (uses M)
*   Path 2: E -> B -> C -> I -> J -> D (uses I)
*   Path 3: E -> B -> C -> O -> P -> D (uses O)
Removing any single node (M, I, or O) would leave at least two of these paths intact.

**Can we disconnect E and D by removing 2 nodes?**
Let's test all combinations of two nodes from {M, I, O}:

1.  **Remove {M, I}:**
    *   Paths through M are blocked.
    *   Paths through I are blocked.
    *   However, paths through O remain open. For example: E -> B -> C -> O -> P -> D.
    *   Therefore, {M, I} is not a cut set.

2.  **Remove {M, O}:**
    *   Paths through M are blocked.
    *   Paths through O are blocked.
    *   However, paths through I remain open. For example: E -> F -> G -> H -> I -> J -> D.
    *   Therefore, {M, O} is not a cut set.

3.  **Remove {I, O}:**
    *   Paths through I are blocked.
    *   Paths through O are blocked.
    *   However, paths through M remain open. For example: E -> M -> J -> D.
    *   Therefore, {I, O} is not a cut set.

Since no set of 1 or 2 nodes can disconnect E and D, and we have identified a valid cut set of 3 nodes ({M, I, O}), the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, O]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between M and B.

1.  **Identify direct connections of the target nodes:**
    *   Node M is directly connected to L, E, and K. This means any path originating from M must first traverse one of these three edges: (M, L), (M, E), or (M, K).
    *   Node B is directly connected to E, H, C, and N.

2.  **Consider a cut set by isolating one of the target nodes:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect M from B is to isolate M completely. To do this, all edges directly connected to M must be removed. These edges are (M, L), (M, E), and (M, K).
    If these three edges are removed, M becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist from M to any other node, including B.
    This cut set has a size of 3.

3.  **Verify if this cut set is minimal (i.e., if a smaller cut set exists):**
    *   **Can we disconnect M and B by removing only 1 edge?** No, because there are multiple distinct paths from M to B (e.g., M-E-B and M-K-X-G-H-B). Removing one edge would leave other paths intact.
    *   **Can we disconnect M and B by removing only 2 edges?** Let's test this by trying to remove any two of the three edges connected to M:
        *   If we remove (M, L) and (M, E), the edge (M, K) remains. From K, there are still paths to B, for example: M - K - X - G - H - B. Thus, M would still be connected to B.
        *   If we remove (M, L) and (M, K), the edge (M, E) remains. From E, there is a direct path to B: M - E - B. Thus, M would still be connected to B.
        *   If we remove (M, E) and (M, K), the edge (M, L) remains. From L, there is a path to B: M - L - E - B. Thus, M would still be connected to B.
    Since removing any two edges directly connected to M leaves M connected to B, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum size:**
    Since 2 edges are not enough, and we found a valid cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

5.  **Consider alternative cut sets (optional, for thoroughness):**
    Could we achieve disconnection by removing 3 edges elsewhere in the graph?
    For example, consider the edges directly connected to B: (E, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B). If we remove any three of these, say (E, B), (H, B), and (C, B), a path from M to B would still exist via N: M - K - X - G - H - I - C - N - B (since (N, B) is not removed). To disconnect B by cutting its direct connections, all four edges (E, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B) would need to be removed, resulting in a cut of size 4. Since we found a cut of size 3, this alternative is not minimal.

Therefore, the minimal cut set consists of the three edges directly connected to node M.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, L), (M, E), (M, K)]

The target nodes are N (black) and G (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between N and G.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node N is connected to: Y, O, B.
    *   Node G is connected to: K, X, H.

2.  **Trace all possible paths from N to G:**
    To systematically find a minimal cut, I'll consider the "entry points" to the G-side of the graph from the N-side. All paths from N to G must eventually pass through one of G's neighbors: H, K, or X.

    Let's list the ways to reach H, K, or X from N:

    *   **Paths to H:**
        *   N - B - H
        *   N - O - P - I - H
        *   N - O - C - I - H
        *   N - O - C - B - H
        (Note: N-Y-O paths are equivalent to N-O paths for reaching H, K, or X, as Y only connects to N and O.)

    *   **Paths to K:**
        *   N - B - E - F - K
        *   N - O - C - B - E - F - K

    *   **Paths to X:**
        *   N - B - E - F - X
        *   N - O - C - B - E - F - X

3.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" edges:**
    To disconnect N from G, we need to cut all these path types. Let's look at the edges that lead into H, K, and X from the N-side of the graph (i.e., not directly from G).

    *   To reach H: Paths use either edge (B, H) or edge (I, H).
    *   To reach K: Paths use edge (F, K).
    *   To reach X: Paths use edge (F, X).

    Notice that both (F, K) and (F, X) originate from node F. Node F itself is reached from N via node E (N-...-E-F).

4.  **Consider a cut set of size 1 or 2:**
    *   **Size 1:** It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect N and G, as there are multiple independent routes (e.g., paths through H, and paths through K/X).
    *   **Size 2:**
        *   If we remove (B, H) and (I, H), we cut all paths to H. However, paths to K (e.g., N-B-E-F-K-G) and paths to X (e.g., N-B-E-F-X-G) would still exist.
        *   If we remove (F, K) and (F, X), we cut all paths to K and X. However, paths to H (e.g., N-B-H-G, N-O-P-I-H-G) would still exist.
        *   If we pick one edge from the H-group and one from the K/X-group (e.g., (B, H) and (F, K)), then paths via (I, H) and paths via (F, X) would still exist.
    Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient.

5.  **Consider a cut set of size 3:**
    We need to cut all paths to H, K, and X.
    *   To cut all paths to H, we must remove both (B, H) and (I, H). (2 edges)
    *   To cut all paths to K and X, we can observe that both K and X are connected to F. All paths from N to F must pass through E (N-...-E-F). Therefore, removing the edge (E, F) will effectively cut off all paths to F, and consequently to K and X, from the N-side of the graph. (1 edge)

    Combining these, a potential cut set is: [(B, H), (I, H), (E, F)].
    Let's verify this cut set:
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks all paths from N that go through B to H.
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks all paths from N that go through I to H.
    *   Removing (E, F) breaks all paths from N that go through E to F, and thus to K and X.
    This set of 3 edges successfully disconnects N from G.

6.  **Alternative cut set of size 3:**
    Another straightforward way to disconnect G is to remove all edges directly connected to it: [(G, K), (G, X), (G, H)]. This also results in a cut of size 3.

Since we have found a cut set of size 3, and we've established that a cut set of size 2 is not possible, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:

Image name: FD-K_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G from N.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of G: X, K, F, E, H.
Next, I identify all direct neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y.

Now, I will trace all possible paths from G to N.

1.  **Paths starting from G through X, K, or F:**
    *   G is connected to X, K, and F.
    *   X is connected to G, K, F.
    *   K is connected to G, X, F.
    *   F is connected to G, X, K.
    None of X, K, or F are connected to any node that leads towards N (B, C, O, Y, or any intermediate nodes like I, J, P, L, M, D). Therefore, any path starting G -> X, G -> K, or G -> F will not reach N. These branches are dead ends for connecting G to N.

2.  **Paths starting from G through E:**
    *   G - E - L - M - J - P - O - N
    *   G - E - M - J - P - O - N
    *   G - E - B - C - N
    *   G - E - B - N

3.  **Paths starting from G through H:**
    *   G - H - I - J - P - O - N
    *   G - H - I - C - N
    *   G - H - B - C - N
    *   G - H - B - N

From this analysis, it is clear that all paths from G to N must pass through either node E or node H. There are no other entry points from G's immediate neighborhood that lead to N.

*   If I remove only node E, all paths through E are broken. However, paths through H would still exist (e.g., G - H - B - N). So, removing E alone is not sufficient.
*   If I remove only node H, all paths through H are broken. However, paths through E would still exist (e.g., G - E - B - N). So, removing H alone is not sufficient.

To completely disconnect G from N, I must remove both E and H.
If E and H are removed:
*   G's remaining neighbors are X, K, F. As established, these do not lead to N.
*   N's remaining neighbors are B, C, O, Y. None of these are connected to X, K, or F.
Therefore, removing both E and H successfully disconnects G from N.

The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the cut set consists of nodes E and H.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['E', 'H'], ['F', 'H']]}

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Analyze Connections from Node L:** Node L has two direct connections: (L, M) and (L, E). Any path originating from L must use one of these two edges.
3.  **Consider Disconnection Strategy:** To completely disconnect L from J, we must ensure that no path exists between them. This means we need to remove edges such that L can no longer reach J.
4.  **Evaluate Minimum Edges:**
    *   If we remove only one edge connected to L (e.g., (L, M)), L would still be connected to E, and from E, there are multiple paths leading to J (e.g., L-E-B-C-I-J, L-E-G-H-I-J, L-E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, etc.). Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient.
    *   This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.
5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    *   Consider removing both edges directly connected to L: (L, M) and (L, E).
    *   If (L, M) is removed, the direct path L-M-J is broken.
    *   If (L, E) is removed, all paths starting from L through E (e.g., L-E-B-..., L-E-G-..., L-E-F-...) are broken.
    *   With both (L, M) and (L, E) removed, node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between L and J.
6.  **Verify Minimality:** Since we established that at least 2 edges are required, and we found a set of 2 edges that achieves the disconnection, this set represents the minimum number of edges.
7.  **Alternative Cut Set of Size 2:** Another valid minimal cut set would be [(L, E), (M, J)]. Removing (L, E) breaks all paths from L through E. Removing (M, J) breaks the direct path L-M-J. Since L only connects to M and E, these two edges would also disconnect L from J.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, M), (L, E)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['E', 'L'], ['L', 'M']]}

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled L and H. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node L to find the shortest path to node H.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(L, [L])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{L}`

2.  **Level 1 (Length 1 paths):**
    *   Dequeue `(L, [L])`.
    *   Neighbors of L: M, E.
    *   Add `(M, [L, M])` to queue. Mark M as visited.
    *   Add `(E, [L, E])` to queue. Mark E as visited.
    *   Current Queue: `[(M, [L, M]), (E, [L, E])]`
    *   Visited: `{L, M, E}`
    *   Neither M nor E is the target node H.

3.  **Level 2 (Length 2 paths):**
    *   Dequeue `(M, [L, M])`.
    *   Neighbors of M: L, F.
    *   L is visited.
    *   Add `(F, [L, M, F])` to queue. Mark F as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(E, [L, E])`.
    *   Neighbors of E: L, F, B.
    *   L is visited.
    *   F is visited (path `[L, E, F]` is also length 2, but we already have a path to F).
    *   Add `(B, [L, E, B])` to queue. Mark B as visited.
    *   Current Queue: `[(F, [L, M, F]), (B, [L, E, B])]`
    *   Visited: `{L, M, E, F, B}`
    *   Neither F nor B is the target node H.

4.  **Level 3 (Length 3 paths):**
    *   Dequeue `(F, [L, M, F])`.
    *   Neighbors of F: M, E, X, G, K.
    *   M, E are visited.
    *   Add `(X, [L, M, F, X])` to queue. Mark X as visited.
    *   Add `(G, [L, M, F, G])` to queue. Mark G as visited.
    *   Add `(K, [L, M, F, K])` to queue. Mark K as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(B, [L, E, B])`.
    *   Neighbors of B: E, H, N, C.
    *   E is visited.
    *   **H is the target node!** The path found is `[L, E, B, H]`. This path has a length of 3.

Since BFS explores the graph level by level, the first time the target node H is reached guarantees that the path found is the shortest. We found a path of length 3.

**Verification of the path [L, E, B, H]:**
*   Is L connected to E? Yes.
*   Is E connected to B? Yes.
*   Is B connected to H? Yes.

All connections are valid.

Final Answer: Length: 3, Path: [L, E, B, H]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are L and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect L from J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node L (black) is connected to E and M.
    *   Node J (black) is connected to M, I, P, and D.

2.  **Consider paths originating from L:**
    Any path from L to J must start by traversing either the edge (L, E) or the edge (L, M).

3.  **Evaluate the possibility of disconnecting with 1 edge:**
    *   If we remove only (L, E), a path from L to J still exists via L-M-J.
    *   If we remove only (L, M), a path from L to J still exists via L-E-H-I-J (and many other paths starting with L-E).
    *   Since removing any single edge incident to L does not disconnect L from J, it is impossible to achieve disconnection with just 1 edge.

4.  **Evaluate the possibility of disconnecting with 2 edges:**
    *   Consider removing both edges directly connected to L: (L, E) and (L, M).
    *   If (L, E) is removed, L can no longer reach E.
    *   If (L, M) is removed, L can no longer reach M.
    *   Since L has no other connections, removing these two edges completely isolates L from the rest of the graph. If L is isolated, no path can exist between L and J.
    *   This cut set, [(L, E), (L, M)], successfully disconnects L from J and consists of 2 edges.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 1 edge is not sufficient, and 2 edges are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, E), (L, M)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['E', 'L'], ['L', 'M']]}

Image name: SEIS_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G and N, without removing G or N themselves.

1.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Nodes directly connected to G: H, F, K.
    *   Nodes directly connected to N: B, C, Y.

2.  **Analyze paths to N:**
    Any path from G to N must eventually reach one of N's direct neighbors (B, C, or Y) before reaching N.
    *   Node Y is only connected to N. There are no paths from G that lead to Y. Therefore, Y is not a critical node for connecting G and N.
    *   This means all paths from G to N must pass through either node B or node C.

3.  **Evaluate potential cut sets:**
    *   **Can a single node disconnect G and N?**
        *   If I remove B, paths through C would still exist (e.g., G-H-I-C-N).
        *   If I remove C, paths through B would still exist (e.g., G-H-B-N).
        *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., H, F, K, I, P, J, M, L, E, O, X, D), there would always be at least one path remaining that goes through B or C. For example, removing H would leave paths like G-F-E-B-N or G-F-M-J-P-O-C-N.
        *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

    *   **Consider a cut set of two nodes:**
        Since all paths from G to N must pass through either B or C, removing both B and C will sever all connections between G and N.
        *   If B is removed, all paths ending in B-N are broken.
        *   If C is removed, all paths ending in C-N are broken.
        *   With both B and C removed, no path can reach N from G.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    We've established that a single node removal is not enough. We found a set of two nodes ([B, C]) that successfully disconnects G and N. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

    (Alternative cut set of size 2: [H, F]. If H is removed, all paths starting G-H-... are broken. If F is removed, all paths starting G-F-... are broken. Additionally, paths starting G-K-X-F-... are broken because F is removed, and X only connects to K and F. Thus, removing H and F would isolate G from the rest of the graph that leads to N. Both [B, C] and [H, F] are valid minimum cut sets.)

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, C]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'H']}

    *   Visited: `{}`
    *   Paths: `{M: [M]}` (stores the path to each node)

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Current node: M
    *   Path: `[M]`

3.  **Level 1:** Explore neighbors of M.
    *   M is connected to L, E, F, J.
    *   Add L, E, F, J to the queue.
    *   Update paths:
        *   `Paths[L] = [M, L]`
        *   `Paths[E] = [M, E]`
        *   `Paths[F] = [M, F]`
        *   `Paths[J] = [M, J]`
    *   Queue: `[L, E, F, J]`

4.  **Level 2:** Explore neighbors of nodes from Level 1.
    *   Dequeue L. Neighbors: M (visited), E (visited). No new unvisited nodes.
    *   Dequeue E. Neighbors: M (visited), L (visited), F (visited), G, B.
        *   Add G, B to the queue.
        *   Update paths:
            *   `Paths[G] = [M, E, G]`
            *   `Paths[B] = [M, E, B]`
    *   Dequeue F. Neighbors: M (visited), E (visited), X, K, G (visited).
        *   Add X, K to the queue.
        *   Update paths:
            *   `Paths[X] = [M, F, X]`
            *   `Paths[K] = [M, F, K]`
    *   Dequeue J. Neighbors: M (visited), I, P, D.
        *   Add I, P, D to the queue.
        *   Update paths:
            *   `Paths[I] = [M, J, I]`
            *   `Paths[P] = [M, J, P]`
            *   `Paths[D] = [M, J, D]`
    *   Queue: `[G, B, X, K, I, P, D]`

5.  **Level 3:** Explore neighbors of nodes from Level 2.
    *   Dequeue G. Neighbors: E (visited), F (visited), X (visited), K (visited), H, B (visited).
        *   Add H to the queue.
        *   Update path: `Paths[H] = [M, E, G, H]`
    *   Dequeue B. Neighbors: E (visited), G (visited), H (visited), C, N.
        *   **Node N is the target node!**
        *   The path to N is `Paths[B] + [N] = [M, E, B, N]`.
        *   The length of this path is 3.

Since BFS guarantees that the first time the target node is reached, the path found is the shortest, we can stop here.

Final Answer: Length: 3, Path: [M, E, B, N]

    *   **Direct Path:** H - B
    *   **Paths through G:**
        *   H - G - F - E - B
        *   H - G - X - F - E - B
    *   **Paths through I:**
        *   H - I - C - B
        *   H - I - P - O - C - B
        *   H - I - P - O - N - B

2.  **Determine the minimum number of edge-disjoint paths:**
    To find the minimum number of edges to remove (edge connectivity), I can look for edge-disjoint paths between H and B. If I find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' edges must be removed.
    *   **Path 1:** H - B (Edges: {(H, B)})
    *   **Path 2:** H - G - F - E - B (Edges: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B)})
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - B (Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)})

    These three paths are indeed edge-disjoint, meaning they do not share any common edges.
    Therefore, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect H from B. A straightforward approach is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    Let's consider the edges directly connected to H: (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I).
    *   If I remove the edge **(H, B)**, the direct path between H and B is broken.
    *   If I remove the edge **(H, G)**, all paths from H that go through G (e.g., H-G-F-E-B, H-G-X-F-E-B) are broken at their origin from H.
    *   If I remove the edge **(H, I)**, all paths from H that go through I (e.g., H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, H-I-P-O-N-B) are broken at their origin from H.

    By removing these three edges: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)], node H becomes completely isolated from all other nodes in the graph, including B. Thus, no path can exist between H and B.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of 3 edges, this cut set is minimal.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]

        *   E - M - F - K - G - H (uses M, F)

        *   E - L - M - F - G - H (uses L, M, F)
        *   E - L - M - F - X - G - H (uses L, M, F)
        *   E - L - M - F - K - G - H (uses L, M, F)
        *   E - L - M - F - K - J - I - H (uses L, M, F)
        All these paths from E via L eventually lead to M, then F.

5.  **Determine Critical Nodes:**
    Let's consider if there's any path from E to H that *does not* pass through B or F.
    *   If a path starts from E, it must go to L, M, F, or B.
    *   If it avoids B and F, it must start E -> L or E -> M.
    *   If E -> L, L is only connected to M (and E). So the path must continue E -> L -> M.
    *   If E -> M, M is connected to L and F. Since F is excluded, the path must continue E -> M -> L.
    *   In both cases, the path reaches M, then L, and cycles between L and M (E - L - M - L - M...). Neither L nor M has any other connections that lead towards H without passing through F (which is excluded) or B (which is excluded).
    *   Therefore, every single path from E to H must pass through either node B or node F.

6.  **Formulate a Cut Set:**
    Since all paths from E to H must pass through either B or F, removing both B and F will guarantee that no path remains between E and H.
    This gives a cut set of {B, F}. The size of this cut set is 2.

7.  **Verify Minimum Size:**
    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 node?**
        *   If we remove B: Paths like E - F - G - H still exist.
        *   If we remove F: Paths like E - B - H still exist.
        *   If we remove G: Paths like E - B - H and E - F - K - J - I - H still exist.
        *   If we remove I: Paths like E - B - H and E - F - G - H still exist.
        *   Removing any other single node would clearly leave multiple paths intact (e.g., removing M leaves E-B-H and E-F-G-H).
    *   Since no single node removal can disconnect E and H, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

8.  **Conclusion:** The cut set {B, F} has a size of 2, and we've established that 1 node is insufficient. Therefore, 2 is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, F]

    I will look for edge-disjoint paths to determine a lower bound for the minimum cut size, based on Menger's Theorem.

    *   **Path 1: E - M - J**
        Edges involved: (E, M), (M, J)

    *   **Path 2: E - P - J**
        Edges involved: (E, P), (P, J)

    *   **Path 3: E - B - H - I - J**
        Edges involved: (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J)
        (An alternative for Path 3 could be E - B - C - I - J, using edges (E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J). Both are valid for demonstrating edge-disjointness from P1 and P2.)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   Edges for Path 1: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   Edges for Path 2: {(E, P), (P, J)}
    *   Edges for Path 3: {(E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J)}

    Upon inspection, these three sets of edges are completely distinct; they share no common edges. This means there are at least three edge-disjoint paths between E and J. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect E and J must be at least 3.

5.  **Propose and verify a cut set of size 3:**
    Since we know at least 3 edges are needed, I will try to find a cut set of exactly 3 edges. A common strategy is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that lead towards the other target node.

    Let's consider removing the edges connecting E to its neighbors that are part of paths to J:
    *   **(E, M)**: This cuts Path 1 (E-M-J).
    *   **(E, P)**: This cuts Path 2 (E-P-J).
    *   **(E, B)**: This cuts all paths originating from E through B (e.g., E-B-H-I-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-H-G-F-P-J, etc.).

    If these three edges [(E, M), (E, P), (E, B)] are removed:
    *   Node E is now only connected to node L.
    *   Node L is connected to E and M. Since (E, M) is removed, L cannot reach J via M through E.
    *   All paths from E to J are broken. E is effectively isolated from J.

    This cut set successfully disconnects E from J and consists of 3 edges. Since we established that a minimum of 3 edges are required, this is the minimal cut.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (E, P), (E, B)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and I. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

2.  **Identify immediate neighbors of E:** E is connected to B, F, M, and L.
    *   Node L is only connected to E and M. This means any path from E that goes through L must then go through M (E -> L -> M -> ...). Therefore, removing M will also sever any paths that would have gone through L.

3.  **Identify immediate neighbors of I:** I is connected to H, C, P, and J.

4.  **Find node-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum cut set size, I will look for the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between E and I. Node-disjoint means they do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than E and I).

    Let's trace some paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - C - I
        (Intermediate nodes: B, C)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H - I
        (Intermediate nodes: F, G, H)
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I
        (Intermediate nodes: M, J, P)

5.  **Verify node-disjoint property:**
    *   Nodes in Path 1: {B, C}
    *   Nodes in Path 2: {F, G, H}
    *   Nodes in Path 3: {M, J, P}
    These three sets of intermediate nodes are completely distinct from each other. Therefore, these are three node-disjoint paths between E and I.

6.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (s-t cut) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

7.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    Considering the immediate neighbors of E (B, F, M, and L which funnels through M), any path from E to I must pass through B, F, or M.
    *   If I remove node B, all paths starting E -> B -> ... -> I are broken.
    *   If I remove node F, all paths starting E -> F -> ... -> I are broken.
    *   If I remove node M, all paths starting E -> M -> ... -> I (including those via L, i.e., E -> L -> M -> ...) are broken.

    Therefore, removing the set of nodes {B, F, M} will disconnect E from I. This is a cut set of size 3.

8.  **Conclusion:** Since the maximum number of node-disjoint paths is 3, and I have found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of nodes required.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, F, M]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E and H. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H. This is equivalent to finding the minimum edge cut between E and H. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from E to H:

1.  **Path 1: E - B - H**
    Edges used: `{(E, B), (B, H)}`

2.  Now, we look for a path from E to H that does not use any of the edges from Path 1.
    **Path 2: E - F - G - H**
    Edges used: `{(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it shares no common edges.

3.  Next, we look for a third path from E to H that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
    Edges used so far: `{(E, B), (B, H), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}`.
    From E, the remaining available edges are `(E, L)` and `(E, M)`.
    From H, the remaining available edge is `(I, H)`.
    Let's try to construct a path from E (via L or M) to I, and then to H.
    Consider the path: **E - M - L - F - K - J - I - H**
    Edges used: `{(E, M), (M, L), (L, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), (I, H)}`
    Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
    *   It does not use `(E, B)` or `(B, H)` (from Path 1).
    *   It does not use `(E, F)`, `(F, G)`, or `(G, H)` (from Path 2).
    All edges in Path 3 are distinct from the edges in Path 1 and Path 2. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, we need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, especially if it has a low degree.
Node H has three incident edges: `(B, H)`, `(G, H)`, and `(I, H)`.
If we remove these three edges, node H becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node E.

Let's verify that removing these edges breaks all identified paths:
*   Path 1 (E - B - H) is broken by removing `(B, H)`.
*   Path 2 (E - F - G - H) is broken by removing `(G, H)`.
*   Path 3 (E - M - L - F - K - J - I - H) is broken by removing `(I, H)`.

Since all paths from E to H must eventually pass through one of H's incident edges to reach H, removing `(B, H)`, `(G, H)`, and `(I, H)` guarantees that no path exists between E and H.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

Step-by-step reasoning.

    *   Node D is connected to J and P.
    *   Node E is connected to M, L, F, and B.

2.  **Analyze paths to D:**
    Any path from E to D must necessarily pass through one of D's direct connections: either the edge (J, D) or the edge (P, D).

3.  **Consider removing edges directly connected to D:**
    *   If we remove the edge (J, D), all paths that go through J to reach D are broken.
    *   If we remove the edge (P, D), all paths that go through P to reach D are broken.
    *   To completely disconnect D from E, both of these edges, (J, D) and (P, D), must be removed.

4.  **Evaluate the size of this cut set:**
    Removing `[(J, D), (P, D)]` results in 2 edges being removed. After these two edges are removed, node D becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node E.

5.  **Check for a smaller cut set (size 1):**
    It is not possible to disconnect D from E by removing only one edge. If only (J, D) is removed, paths through P (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-D) would still exist. If only (P, D) is removed, paths through J (e.g., E-M-J-D) would still exist. Therefore, a cut of size 1 is not sufficient.

6.  **Check for alternative cut sets of size 2:**
    To disconnect D, we must sever all paths leading to J and all paths leading to P.
    *   Paths to J (excluding (J, D)): The only edge leading to J is (M, J).
    *   Paths to P (excluding (P, D)): The edges leading to P are (O, P) and (I, P).
    If we try to cut paths *before* they reach J or P, we would need to remove:
    *   (M, J) to stop paths to J.
    *   (O, P) AND (I, P) to stop paths to P.
    This alternative cut set would be `[(M, J), (O, P), (I, P)]`, which consists of 3 edges. This is more than the 2 edges identified earlier.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

    *   K is also connected to F, so it's a dead end for moving towards J.

2.  **Paths starting F -> X:**

    *   X is also connected to K and F, which are not on the direct path to J.

3.  **Paths starting F -> G:**
    *   F -> G. From G, the only node that leads towards the right side of the graph (where J is located) is H. (K and X are to the left/up from G).
    *   So, any path from F that goes through G *must* then go through H to proceed towards J.

4.  **Paths starting F -> E:**
    *   F -> E. From E, the only node that leads towards the right side of the graph (where J is located) is B. (M and L are downwards from E).
    *   So, any path from F that goes through E *must* then go through B to proceed towards J.

**Conclusion on path structure:**
Based on the analysis above, every single path from F to J must pass through either node H (if it goes via G) or node B (if it goes via E). There are no other "gateways" from the left side of the graph (F's side) to the right side (J's side) without passing through H or B.

Therefore, to disconnect F from J, both H and B must be removed.

**Verification:**
*   If H is removed, all paths that go F -> G -> H -> ... are broken. However, paths like F -> E -> B -> C -> I -> J would still exist. So, removing H alone is not enough.
*   If B is removed, all paths that go F -> E -> B -> ... are broken. However, paths like F -> G -> H -> I -> J would still exist. So, removing B alone is not enough.
*   If both H and B are removed, then:
    *   Any path starting F -> G is forced to go through H, which is removed.
    *   Any path starting F -> E is forced to go through B, which is removed.
    *   Since all paths from F to J must pass through either H or B, removing both will ensure no path remains.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2. The cut set consists of nodes H and B.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I. This is a minimum cut problem, which by Menger's theorem is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I.

1.  **Identify potential paths from G to I:**
    Let's systematically look for paths starting from G and ending at I.

2.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths:**
    We will try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible. If we find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least 'k'.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): G - H - I**
        Edges: `(G,H)`, `(H,I)`

    *   **Path 2 (P2): G - K - J - I**
        Edges: `(G,K)`, `(K,J)`, `(J,I)`
        (P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint as they use completely different sets of edges.)

    *   **Path 3 (P3): G - F - E - B - C - I**
        Edges: `(G,F)`, `(F,E)`, `(E,B)`, `(B,C)`, `(C,I)`
        (Let's check if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.
        P1 edges: `(G,H)`, `(H,I)`
        P2 edges: `(G,K)`, `(K,J)`, `(J,I)`
        P3 edges: `(G,F)`, `(F,E)`, `(E,B)`, `(B,C)`, `(C,I)`
        All three paths use distinct sets of edges. Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are edge-disjoint.)

3.  **Determine the lower bound for the cut size:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Now we need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect G and I. A common strategy is to pick one edge from each of the identified edge-disjoint paths.

    Let's consider the following edges:
    *   From P1: `(H,I)` (This edge directly connects H to I)
    *   From P2: `(J,I)` (This edge directly connects J to I)
    *   From P3: `(F,E)` (This edge is a critical link early in the path from G-F to I)

    Proposed Cut Set: `[(H,I), (J,I), (F,E)]`

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    We must ensure that after removing these three edges, no path exists between G and I.

    *   **Removing (H,I):** This directly breaks Path 1 (G-H-I). Any other path that might have used (H,I) is also broken.
    *   **Removing (J,I):** This directly breaks Path 2 (G-K-J-I). It also breaks any other path that uses (J,I), such as G-X-K-J-I. Furthermore, it breaks paths like G-K-J-P-I and G-X-K-J-P-I, as they rely on J to reach P and then I.
    *   **Removing (F,E):** This breaks all paths that originate from G, pass through F, and then attempt to reach E. This includes:
        *   G-F-E-B-C-I (P3 is broken)
        *   G-F-E-B-N-C-I
        *   G-F-E-B-N-O-P-I
        *   Any path that goes G-F-M-L-E-... (e.g., G-F-M-L-E-B-C-I, G-F-M-L-E-B-N-O-P-I) is also broken because E cannot be reached from F (or M-L-E branch) if (F,E) is removed.

    By removing these three edges, all identified paths and any other possible paths from G to I are broken. For example, G can still reach X, K, F, H. But from H, I is disconnected. From K, J is disconnected from I. From F, E is disconnected from F, so the entire branch through E is cut off. From X, it can go to K, but then J is disconnected from I.

    Therefore, the cut set `[(H,I), (J,I), (F,E)]` successfully disconnects G and I.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths (meaning the minimum cut is at least 3) and we found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.
